Gibbs Dynamics for Fractional Nonlinear Schrödinger Equations with Weak Dispersion
Rui Liang and Yuzhao Wang

Abstract
. We consider the Cauchy problem for the one-dimensional periodic cubic nonlinear fractional Schrödinger equation (FNLS) with initial data distributed via its associated Gibbs measure. We construct global strong solutions with the flow property for the FNLS on the support of the Gibbs measure in the full dispersive range, thus resolving a question proposed by Sun-Tzvetkov (2021). As a byproduct, we prove the invariance of the Gibbs measure and almost sure global well-posedness for FNLS.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q55, 37K99

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we study the problem of invariant Gibbs measure for the fractional cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation (FNLS) on the torus
\mathbb{T}
:

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u-{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}u=\pm|u|^{2}u,\\
u(0)=u_{0},\end{cases}\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}

(1.1)

where
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
is the one-dimensional torus
and
{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}=(-\partial_{xx})^{\alpha/2}
is the
spatial fractional derivative defined as the Fourier multiplier
\mathcal{F}_{x}({\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}f)(k):=|k|^{\alpha}\widehat{f}(k)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We use the symbol
\pm
to indicate the type of nonlinearity in FNLS (
1.1
), which can be either defocusing (
+
) or focusing (
-
).
The parameter
\alpha>0
measures the dispersion effect in FNLS (
1.1
).
The dispersion becomes stronger as
\alpha
increases.
When
\alpha=2
, FNLS (
1.1
) reduces to the well-known cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS), which models nonlinear phenomena in optics and plasma physics; see
[
40
]
for more details.
When
\alpha\neq 2
, FNLS (
1.1
) arises in fractional quantum mechanics
[
27
]
, and also in the water wave models
[
25
]
.
Quantum mechanics can be seen as the theory of functionals on measure generated by the Brownian motions in the path-integral approach; see
[
14
,
23
]
.
Fractional quantum mechanics is a generalization of quantum mechanics by replacing the Brownian motions with Lévy processes
[
27
]
.
Moreover, it is shown in
[
26
]
that FNLS (
1.1
) describes the continuum limit of lattice interactions when
\alpha\in(1,2]
. Specifically, the case when
1<\alpha<2
corresponds to the long-range lattice interactions, while the case when
\alpha=2
corresponds to the short-range or quick-decaying interactions.
We also note that the cubic nonlinear half-wave equation (
\alpha=1
) has various physical applications, such as wave turbulence
[
30
,
15
]
and gravitational collapse
[
21
]
. However, when
\alpha=1
, the equation (
1.1
) is no longer dispersive.
In this paper, we focus on FNLS (
1.1
) for
\alpha\in(1,2)
, where the dispersion is weaker than that of the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) (for (
1.1
) with
\alpha=2
).

The equation (
1.1
) is a Hamiltonian PDE with the conserved Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H(u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\big|{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha/2}u\big|^{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}|u|^{4}\bigg)dx.

(1.2)

The dynamics of the equation (
1.1
) also preserves the mass:

\displaystyle M(u)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{2}dx.

(1.3)

The well-posedness of FNLS (
1.1
) in the low regularity setting has
been extensively studied. If
\alpha=2
, (
1.1
) is well-posed in

L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
in the deterministic sense
[
1
]
. However, when

\alpha<2
, where the equation is less dispersive, the deterministic local
well-posedness in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
is not available. Nevertheless, it has been proved in
[
16
,
13
]
that FNLS (
1.1
) with
1<\alpha<2
is
locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s\geq\frac{2-\alpha}{4}
.
The main purpose of this paper is to study FNLS (
1.1
) from a probabilistic point of view.
In particular, we focus on the invariance of the Gibbs measure (
1.5
) under the flow of (
1.1
), where the Gibbs measure is a probability measure on distributions on
\mathbb{T}
. See Subsection
1.1
for a precise definition.

The existence and invariance of Gibbs measures for nonlinear PDEs are fascinating topics that have attracted a lot of attention in recent years
[
22
,
28
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
44
,
45
,
31
,
32
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
37
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
36
,
35
,
41
,
42
,
29
,
9
]
. Here we did not aim to cover all the relevant literature on this topic.
We would like to acknowledge the pioneering works of Bourgain
[
2
,
3
]
that inspire many subsequent studies on the invariant measure for Hamiltonian PDEs, especially those by Burq and Tzvetkov
[
44
,
45
,
10
,
11
]
, that popularized this line of research. One of the main motivations for studying such measures is to understand the long-time behaviour of solutions to nonlinear PDEs, especially those that exhibit chaotic or turbulent phenomena.
One such example is the famous question posed by Zakharov; see
[
22
]
.
“Numerical experiments demonstrated [that the 1-d periodic cubic NLW] possesses
the ‘returning’ property, i.e. solutions appear to be very close to the initial state
\cdots
, after some time of rather chaotic evolution. The problem is to explain this phenomenon.”
The key ingredients to explain this phenomenon are the existence of an invariant Gibbs measure and the flow property for the dynamics,
which enable the use of Poincaré recurrence theorem. However, proving the existence and invariance of such a measure is highly nontrivial, as it requires overcoming several analytical and probabilistic challenges.
See
[
22
,
28
,
2
,
3
,
9
]
and references therein for further discussion.
We also refer readers to
[
33
]
for a nice review of other invariant measures.

For the NLS case, i.e. (
1.1
) with
\alpha=2
, Lebowitz-Rose-Speer
[
28
]
considered the Gibbs measure of the form

\displaystyle d\rho=Z^{-1}e^{-\beta H(u)}du

(1.4)

where
Z
is a normalization constant and
H(u)
is the Hamiltonian given in (
1.2
).
In particular,
they showed that the Gibbs measure (
1.4
) is a well-defined probability measure on
H^{\frac{1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T})
for the defocusing case; while for the focusing case, their result only holds with the
L^{2}
-cutoff
\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq K\}}
for some
K>0
.
Bourgain
[
2
]
proved the Gibbs measure (
1.4
) is invariant under the flow of NLS and global well-posedness almost surely on the statistical ensemble.

For the fractional NLS (
1.1
) with
\alpha\in(1,2)
, the invariance of the Gibbs measure and the “returning” property for the dynamics are not yet fully understood.
When
\alpha>\frac{4}{3}
, these problems can be settled by using the deterministic theory
[
16
,
13
]
since the local well-posedness holds on the support of the Gibbs measure.
To go beyond the threshold
\frac{4}{3}
, Sun-Tzvetkov
[
41
]
exploited a probabilistic argument, where they managed to handle the case
\alpha>\frac{6}{5}
.
When
\alpha\in(1,\frac{6}{5}]
, they also proved the convergence of the Galerkin approximation scheme for the FNLS by using the Bourgain-Bulut approach
[
5
,
6
,
7
]
.
However, this argument is insufficient to show the flow property for the limiting dynamics, thus preventing us from applying the Poincaré recurrence theorem.
Recently, Sun-Tzvetkov
[
42
]
further improved their results in
[
41
]
by using the theory of random averaging operators introduced by Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
18
]
, and extended the range of
\alpha
to
\alpha>\frac{31-\sqrt{233}}{14}
. They also conjectured the existence of global strong solutions to (
1.1
) for all
\alpha>1
(see
[
42
, Question 1.1]
).

The main goal of this paper is to affirmatively answer Sun-Tzvetkov’s question.
Specifically, we build strong solutions, satisfying the flow property, to FNLS (
1.1
) in the full range
\alpha>1
.
The range
\alpha>1
is also expected to be optimal. As a matter of fact, we shall show that there is a dramatic change in the regularity of the second Picard iterate between
\alpha>1
and
\alpha=1
. See Theorem
1.8
for further discussion.

Before proceeding, we recall the notion of Gibbs measure.

1.1.
Gibbs measures

The Gibbs measure, denoted by
d\rho
, can be formally written as

\displaystyle d\rho=Z^{-1}e^{-H(u)-M(u)}du,

(1.5)

where
Z
is a normalization constant. Here,
H(u)
and
M(u)
are the Hamiltonian and mass of (
1.1
) given in (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), respectively.
Also,
du
is formally an infinite-dimensional Lebesgue measure, which does not make sense as it is. We should understand
du
as part of the Gaussian measure; see (
1.6
) in the following.
As both Hamiltonian and mass are conserved along the dynamics of (
1.1
), and the volume
du
is also formally invariant, it is expected that Gibbs measure (
1.5
) is invariant under the dynamics of (
1.1
).
By adding the mass term to (
1.5
), we avoid some technical issues at zero frequency.
We may define the Gibbs measure
d\rho
as an absolutely continuous
measure with respect to the following massive Gaussian measure:

\displaystyle d\mu=e^{-\int_{\mathbb{T}}(|{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha/2}u|^{2}+|u|^{2})dx}du=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{\alpha}|u_{k}|^{2}}du_{k}d\overline{u}_{k},

(1.6)

where
u_{k}
is the
k
-th Fourier coefficient of
u
.
The above Gaussian measure given in (
1.6
) is the induced probability measure under the map

\omega\longmapsto u_{0}^{\omega}(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{g_{k}(\omega)}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}e^{ikx}.

(1.7)

Here
[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]:=(1+|k|^{\alpha})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
and
(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent standard complex-valued Gaussian
random variables on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
.
One notes that
[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]
depends on
\alpha
in a non-essential way and
[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]\sim\langle k\rangle
; see (
1.44
).
Typical elements on the support of
\mu
can be represented as the random Fourier series given in (
1.7
).
An easy computation shows that

\displaystyle u^{\omega}_{0}\in H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T})\setminus H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})\textup{ almost surely, }

(1.8)

where

H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T}):=\bigcap_{s<\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}H^{s}(\mathbb{T}).

With the above notations,
the defocusing Gibbs measure
\rho
can be recast as the following weighted
Gaussian measure

\displaystyle d\rho=Z^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{4}dx}d\mu.

(1.9)

When
\alpha>1
, we note that
\|u\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T})}<\infty
almost surely with respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu
. Thus, the defocusing Gibbs measure (
1.9
) is a well-defined probability measure on
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s<\frac{\alpha-1}{2}
, absolutely continuous with respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu
.
When
\alpha\leq 1
, one has
\|u\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T})}=\infty
almost surely, thus a renormalization is needed.
We refer the readers to
[
41
, Section 1.3]
for further discussion.
On the other hand, the focusing Gibbs measure formally given by

d\rho=Z^{-1}e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{4}dx}d\mu

cannot be normalized as a probability measure since

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{4}dx}\Big]=\infty.

Inspired by Lebowitz-Rose-Speer
[
28
]
and Oh-Sosoe-Tolomeo
[
37
]
,
the authors
[
29
]
considered the following focusing Gibbs measure with a cutoff,

\displaystyle d\rho=Z^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq K\}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|u|^{4}dx}d\mu,

(1.10)

associated with the focusing FNLS (
1.1
).
In particular, the authors showed that the Gibbs measure (
1.10
) is a probability measure for any finite cutoff size
K
, provided
\alpha>1
.
See
[
29
]
for further discussion.

1.2.
Gauge transform

Given a solution
u\in C([-T,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
to (
1.1
),
we introduce the following invertible gauge transform

u(t)\longmapsto\mathcal{G}(u)(t):=e^{2it\fint|u|^{2}dx}u(t),

(1.11)

where

\fint f(x)dx:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)dx

denotes the integration with respect to the normalized Lebesgue measure
(2\pi)^{-1}dx
on
\mathbb{T}
.
A direct computation with the mass conservation shows that the gauged function, which we still denote by
u
, solves the following renormalized defocusing FNLS:

i\partial_{t}u-{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}u=\Big(|u|^{2}-2\fint|u|^{2}dx\Big)u.

(1.12)

Note that the gauge transform
\mathcal{G}
in (
1.11
) is invertible in
C([-T,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
. In particular, we can freely convert solutions to
(
1.1
) into solutions to (
1.12
) and vice-versa as long as
they are in
C([-T,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
.
By rewriting (
1.12
)
in the Duhamel formulation, we have

u(t)=S(t)u_{0}^{\omega}-i\int^{t}_{0}S(t-t^{\prime})\mathcal{N}(u)(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},

(1.13)

where
S(t)=e^{-it{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}}
denotes
the linear evolution and

\mathcal{N}(u)=\Big(|u|^{2}-2\fint|u|^{2}dx\Big)u.

We decompose the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}(u)
into non-resonant and resonant parts i.e.

\mathcal{N}(u)=\mathcal{Q}(u,u,u)+\mathcal{R}(u,u,u),

where the trilinear forms are defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}(u,v,w)(t,x)&:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\Gamma(k)}u_{k_{1}}(t)\overline{v_{k_{2}}(t)}w_{k_{3}}(t)e^{i(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3})x};\\
\mathcal{R}(u,v,w)(t,x)&:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}(t)\overline{v_{k}(t)}w_{k}(t)e^{ikx}.\end{split}

(1.14)

Here
u_{k},v_{k},w_{k}
denote the spatial Fourier transform of
u,v,w
respectively, and
\Gamma(k)
denotes the hyperplane of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma(k):=\left\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3};k=k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}\text{ and }k_{1},k_{3}\neq k_{2}\right\}.

(1.15)

When all the arguments are the same, we simply denote by
\mathcal{Q}(u)=\mathcal{Q}(u,u,u)
and
\mathcal{R}(u)=\mathcal{R}(u,u,u)
. The term

\mathcal{Q}(u)
denotes the non-resonant part of the renormalized
nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}(u)
, and
\mathcal{R}(u)
denotes the resonant
part. Then, we have

\mathcal{N}(u)=\mathcal{Q}(u)+\mathcal{R}(u).

Remark 1.1
.

When
\alpha>1
, the local-in-time solution to (
1.13
) we construct will be in
H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T})
, almost surely.
See Theorem
1.2
below.
Thus the solution to (
1.13
) lies in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, where the gauge transform

\mathcal{G}
is invertible; i.e. the renormalized FNLS (
1.12
)
is equivalent to the original FNLS (
1.1
).
The use of gauge transformation (
1.11
) removes some troublesome resonances, which improves the
main counting estimates in Lemma
2.5
and thus the tensor
estimates in Lemma
2.12
.

1.3.
Main results

In what follows, we consider the Cauchy problem of defocusing FNLS (
1.12
) with
Gaussian random data
u_{0}^{\omega}
given in (
1.7
).
Let
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\cup\{1/2\}
be a dyadic number,
define projections
\Pi_{N}
such that

\displaystyle(\Pi_{N}f)_{k}=\mathbf{1}_{\langle k\rangle\leq N}\cdot f_{k},

(1.16)

and
\Delta_{N}:=\Pi_{N}-\Pi_{N/2}
.
In particular, we note

\displaystyle(\Pi_{1/2}f)_{k}=0.

(1.17)

We start with a truncated version of (
1.12
), with truncated random
initial data

\begin{cases}\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u_{N}-{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}u_{N}=\Pi_{N}\bigg[\bigg(|u_{N}|^{2}-2{{\fint|u_{N}|^{2}dx}}\bigg)u_{N}\bigg],\\
\displaystyle u_{N}(0)=\Pi_{N}u^{\omega}_{0}=\sum_{\langle k\rangle\leq N}\frac{g_{k}(\omega)}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}e^{ikx},\end{cases}\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.

(1.18)

From (
1.17
) and (
1.18
),
we also note that

\displaystyle u_{1/2}=0.

In this paper, we show that (
1.12
) admits strong solutions, which are the unique limits of solutions to (
1.12
) and satisfying the flow property.
It follows from
[
43
]
(or see
[
41
, Theorem 4]
) that (
1.18
) is globally well-posed with truncated (and thus smooth) initial data.

Our first goal
is to construct local-in-time solutions to (
1.12
) almost surely
with respect to the random initial data (
1.7
), which is addressed in
the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\alpha>1
and
u_{0}^{\omega}
be as in (
1.7
).
Let
u_{N}
be the solution to the truncated system (
1.18
).
Then for
0<T\ll 1
,
there exists a set
\Omega_{T}\subset\Omega
with
\mathbf{P}(\Omega_{T}^{c})\leq C_{\theta}e^{-T^{-\theta}}
, where
\theta>0
is a small constant determined by
\alpha
, such that for
\omega\in\Omega_{T}
, the sequence
\{u_{N}\}
converges to a limit
u
in
C([-T,T];H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T}))
. The limit
u(t)
solves (
1.12
).

Remark 1.3
.

Theorem
1.2
has been shown for
\alpha>\frac{31-\sqrt{233}}{14}\approx 1.124
in
[
42
]
by using the theory of random averaging operators developed by Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
18
]
, with a precursor
[
8
]
.
In what follows, we only focus on the range
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
The random averaging operator can be seen as the dispersive version of the well-known paracontrolled distribution method developed by Gubinelli-Imkeller-Perkowski
[
24
]
.
The key observation is to decompose the solution into a combination of linear, para-linear, and nonlinear parts, such that the para-linear term (or para-controlled term) possesses a randomness structure and the nonlinear part is smoother.

Remark 1.4
.

The solution
u
constructed in Theorem
1.2
is the unique limit of canonical smooth approximations; i.e. the limit
u
obtained in Theorem
1.2
does not depend on the choice of ‘good’ approximations of (
1.12
). For further discussion on this issue, see
[
18
, Remark 1.6 (3)]
and
[
19
, Remark 1.4]
.
We also refer the readers to
[
41
]
for some different approximations.

Remark 1.5
.

The main difficulty in establishing Theorem
1.2
comes from the weak dispersion when
\alpha
is small.
Besides the random averaging operator, we need to exploit several new ideas and techniques to overcome the difficulty arising from the weaker dispersion. First of all, we employ the random tensor estimates introduced in
[
19
,
20
]
, which allow us to simplify the multilinear estimates and exploit more refined counting results. See Subsection
2.3
, Subsection
4.1
, and Subsection
5.1
for more details. Secondly, we improve the counting estimates by exploiting some key properties of the random structure. In particular, we show that the worst-case scenario of bad countings does not happen when considering the interaction between high and low frequencies, which is one of the crucial observations. We make crucial use of the decay fact in the basic counting Lemma
2.5
. This enables us to reduce two dimensions in our key counting estimates; see Lemma
2.10
. This contrasts sharply with the half-wave equation, where at most one dimension can be reduced. See Theorem
1.8
. We also use the
\Gamma
-condition to enhance some of the counting arguments. See Subsection
2.2
for further discussion. Last but not least, we take advantage of the crucial cancellation that arises from the conservation structure of the random averaging operator, namely the unitary property. See Subsection
3.3
for further discussion. It is important to note that the counting arguments become more complicated in higher dimensions. Specifically, the
\Gamma
-condition does not eliminate the impact of high-frequency terms due to the inner product structure. We plan to address these issues in our future work.

Theorem
1.2
shows that the FNLS (
1.1
) is almost surely
locally well-posed with the random initial data (
1.7
). To extend the local solution globally, one may adapt Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
2
,
3
]
. See also
[
18
, Section 6]
for a detailed argument in the random averaging operator setting. More precisely, we use the invariance of the
fractional Gibbs measure under the finite-dimensional approximation of the
FNLS flow to obtain a uniform control on the solutions and then apply a PDE
approximation argument to extend the local solutions to (
1.1
)
obtained from Theorem
1.2
to global ones. The proof of Theorem
1.2
, using the theory of random averaging operators, also implies the stability of the truncated solution map. See
[
18
, Section 5]
. As a consequence, we obtain the group property for the flow map and the invariance of the fractional Gibbs measure under the resulting global flow of the FNLS (
1.1
).

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\alpha>1
. Then, the defocusing renormalized FNLS (
1.12
) on
\mathbb{T}
is globally well-posed almost surely with respect to the
Gibbs measure
\rho
in (
1.5
). Moreover, the flow maps satisfy the usual group (flow) property and keep the Gibbs measure
d\rho
invariant.

Once we have Theorem
1.2
, the proof of Theorem
1.6
is standard by now, thus we omit the proof.
See Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
18
, Section 6]
for a proof of the 2D NLS case; also see Bourgain
[
2
,
3
]
, Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
20
]
, and Sun-Tzvetkov
[
41
]
for more discussion.
The crucial point is that, in the proof of Theorem
1.2
, we established a probabilistic local well-posedness result with a fine structure of the solution of (
1.1
). See Subsection
1.4
for further discussion.
In what follows, we shall focus on the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 1.7
.

Theorem
1.6
is for FNLS (
1.1
) (or (
1.12
)) with the defocusing nonlinearity
|u|^{2}u
.
The local-in-time theory still holds if we replace the defocusing
nonlinearity with the focusing one
-|u|^{2}u
. In the focusing case, to use Bourgain’s invariant measure argument extending local-in-time
dynamics globally, one needs to construct the associated focusing Gibbs measure. The authors
[
29
]
constructed the focusing Gibbs measure for (
1.1
). See also
[
38
]
for a different construction of the focusing Gibbs measure from quantum many-body mechanics.

As pointed out by Sun-Tzvetkov
[
42
]
, the second Picard iterate enjoys some smoothing effect when
\alpha>1
.
We further observe that the high-high interaction of the second Picard iterate exhibits an even stronger smoothing property; see Theorem
1.8
(i) and also Subsection
5.2.1
, which plays an important role in our analysis. However, when
\alpha=1
, the second Picard iterate fails to gain any smoothing; see Theorem
1.8
(ii).
To illustrate these ideas, we consider the following truncated version of the second Picard iterate of high-high interaction.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define the following second Picard iterate

\displaystyle Z^{(2)}_{N}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-t^{\prime}){\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}}\Pi_{N}\bigg[\bigg(|z_{N}(t^{\prime})|^{2}-2\fint|z_{N}(t^{\prime})|^{2}dx\bigg)z_{N}(t^{\prime})\bigg]dt^{\prime},

(1.19)

where

z_{N}(t)=\sum_{N^{1-\delta}<k<N}\frac{g_{k}(\omega)}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}e^{it|k|^{\alpha}+ikx}

is the truncation of the random linear solution.
In (
1.19
), we only see the high-high interactions, i.e.
N_{\med}\gtrsim N_{\max}^{1-\delta}
.
The following theorem shows a sharp contrast of the smoothing property of (
1.19
) for
\alpha>1
and for
\alpha=1
, respectively.
More precisely, we have the following result.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
Z^{(2)}_{N}(t)
be defined by (
1.19
) with
N\gg 1
and
|t|\sim 1
.

(i)

When
\alpha>1
, there exists
\delta>0
, such that
(\mathbb{E}[\|Z^{(2)}_{N}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}])^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}-}
.

(ii)

When
\alpha=1
, we have

(\mathbb{E}[\|Z^{(2)}_{N}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})})^{\frac{1}{2}}\gtrsim\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}(\log N)^{\frac{3}{2}}
with the constant independent of
\delta
.

Remark 1.9
.

Theorem
1.8
shows a dramatic change in the regularity of the second Picard iterate between
\alpha>1
and
\alpha=1
. In particular, Theorem
1.8
quantifies the phase transition of the smoothing effect for Picard iterates at
\alpha=1
.
As the smoothing property of the second Picard iterate plays a crucial role in the proof of Theorem
1.2
, Theorem
1.8
also implies that the strategy in proving Theorem
1.2
does not work for the half-wave equation, i.e. (
1.12
) with
\alpha=1
.

Remark 1.10
.

Picard iterate’s lack of smoothing property for other weakly dispersive models has been observed. Oh
[
34
]
considered the Szegö equation and proved that the first nontrivial Picard iterate does not gain regularity compared to the initial data. See also Camps-Gassot-Ibrahim
[
12
]
for a similar observation for the cubic Schrödinger half-wave equation.

1.4.
Main ideas

We explain the key ideas in proving Theorem
1.2
, i.e. how to construct local-in-time solutions to (
1.1
) with initial data distributed according to the Gibbs measure.
We note that the Gibbs measure
d\rho
in (
1.9
) (or in (
1.10
)) is absolutely continuous with respect to the Gaussian measure
d\mu
in (
1.6
).
Therefore, to prove Theorem
1.2
, it only suffices to consider (
1.1
) with initial data distributed according to
d\mu
, i.e.
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}^{\omega}
given in (
1.7
).
The main difference between the weakly dispersive case
\alpha\in(1,2)
and the standard case
\alpha=2
comes from the counting estimates. For instance, the values of

|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-|k_{2}|^{\alpha}+|k_{3}|^{\alpha}-|k|^{\alpha}

may be dense in an interval of size
1
,
under the constraint of
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\Gamma(k)
. This causes the loss of regularity in establishing linear and multilinear estimates regarding solutions to (
1.12
).

1.4.1.
The Ansatz

In this subsection, we recall the theory of random averaging operators recently developed by Deng-Nahmod-Yue
[
18
]
.
The idea is to include the high-low interactions in the ansatz, and write them as a low-frequency operator applied to the high-frequency.
See
[
18
]
for further discussion.

To construct the solution to (
1.18
) locally,
we introduce a time cutoff.
Let
\eta
be any Schwartz function such that
\eta(t)=1
for
|t|\leq 1
and
\eta(t)=0
for
|t|\geq 2
.
To simplify the notation, we will denote by

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\eta_{T}(t):=\eta\bigg(\frac{t}{T}\bigg),

(1.20)

for some
T\ll 1
to be determined later.
Applying the interaction representation to the unknown
u_{N}(t)
, still denoted by
u_{N}(t)
,

u_{N}(t)=e^{it{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}}u_{N}(t),

the Duhamel formulation of (
1.18
), restricted to
|t|\leq T
, becomes

\begin{split}u_{N}(t)=&\chi(t)\Pi_{N}u^{\omega}_{0}-i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(u_{N})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}+i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{R}(u_{N})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},\end{split}

(1.21)

where
\mathcal{R}
is as in (
1.14
) attaching a factor
\chi(t)
,
and
\mathcal{M}(u)=\mathcal{M}(u,u,u)
is the trilinear form defined by

\mathcal{M}(u,v,w)_{k}(t)={{\chi(t)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k\\
k_{2}\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\}\end{subarray}}e^{it\Phi}\cdot u_{k_{1}}(t)\overline{v_{k_{2}}}(t)w_{k_{3}}(t),

(1.22)

where

\Phi=\Phi(\bar{k}):=|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-|k_{2}|^{\alpha}+|k_{3}|^{\alpha}-|k|^{\alpha}.

(1.23)

In what follows,
we focus on the formulation (
1.21
) in proving Theorem
1.2
. We also note that
u_{1/2}=0
.

Let
y_{N}
be as

y_{N}=u_{N}-u_{N/2}.

(1.24)

Then from (
1.21
) we see that
y_{N}
in (
1.24
) satisfies

\begin{split}&y_{N}(t)=\chi(t)F_{N}-i\sum_{N_{\max}=N}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-i\sum_{N_{\max}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Delta_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt+i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}(\Pi_{N}\mathcal{R}(u_{N})-\Pi_{N/2}\mathcal{R}(u_{N/2}))(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},\end{split}

(1.25)

where

F_{N}=\Delta_{N}u_{0}^{\omega}=\Pi_{N}u^{\omega}_{0}-\Pi_{N/2}u^{\omega}_{0},

(1.26)

and
N_{\max}=\max(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})
.
To construct
y_{N}
perturbatively,
the main difficulty comes from the
low-low-high interactions of the second term in (
1.25
).
To remove this bad interaction, we introduce a random averaging operator.
To be more precise, let
0<\delta\ll 1
be determined later, and denote by

\displaystyle L_{N}=L(N):=\max\{L\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}};L<N^{1-\delta}\}.

(1.27)

We define
\psi_{N,L_{N}}
to be the solution to the linear equation

\psi_{N,L_{N}}(t)=\chi(t)F_{N}-2i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(u_{L_{N}},u_{L_{N}},\psi_{N,L_{N}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},

(1.28)

which is expected to consist of bad high-low interactions from
y_{N}
.
We note that the local-in-time existence of solutions to (
1.28
) is guaranteed by a fixed point argument, as the norm of the random averaging operator (
1.33
) can be made small provided
T\ll 1
; see (
3.20
).
We then further decompose
y_{N}
into

\displaystyle y_{N}=\psi_{N,L_{N}}+z_{N}.

(1.29)

It is, therefore, expected that the remainder
z_{N}
behaves
better than
y_{N}
.
From (
1.25
), (
1.27
), (
1.28
), and (
1.29
), we note that the remainder

z_{N}
satisfies

\begin{split}&z_{N}(t)=-i\sum_{N_{\max}=N_{\med}=N}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-2i\sum_{N_{1},N_{2}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},z_{N})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-2i\sum_{L_{N}<N_{\med}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},\psi_{N,L_{N}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-i\sum_{N_{1},N_{3}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},z_{N},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-i\sum_{N_{1},N_{3}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},\psi_{N,L_{N}},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt-i\sum_{N_{\max}\leq N/2}\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Delta_{N}\mathcal{M}(y_{N_{1}},y_{N_{2}},y_{N_{3}})(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 45.52458pt+i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}(\Pi_{N}\mathcal{R}(u_{N})-\Pi_{N/2}\mathcal{R}(u_{N/2}))(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(1.30)

The equation (
1.30
) will be our main concern when constructing
z_{N}
.
The main advantage of this formulation is the appearance of the lower bounds
of
N_{\med}
in the third term of the right-hand side
of (
1.30
), i.e.
L_{N}<N_{\med}\leq N/2
. Therefore, there is essentially no low-low-high interaction from this formulation. This lower bound of
N_{\med}
comes from the removal of
\psi_{N,L_{N}}
from
y_{N}
.
In what follows, we shall construct
z_{N}
in an induction manner. See Section
3
for further discussion.

1.4.2.
The random averaging operator

Let us have a closer look at
\psi_{N,L_{N}}
defined in (
1.28
).
For any
0\leq L\leq N/2
of dyadic
L\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\cup\{0\}
, consider the linear equation for
\Psi
:

\partial_{t}\Psi(t)=-2i\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(u_{L},u_{L},\Psi)(t),

(1.31)

where
u_{L}
are solutions to (
1.21
) with
N
being replaced by
L
, and
\mathcal{M}
is the trilinear form given in (
1.22
). We remark that
u_{L}(t)
, the solution to (
1.18
), uniquely exists for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
If (
1.31
) has initial
data
\Psi(0)=\Psi_{0}
, then we can rewrite (
1.31
) as

\Psi(t)-\chi(t)\Psi_{0}=\mathcal{P}^{N,L}[\Psi](t),

(1.32)

for
|t|\leq T
,
where
\mathcal{P}^{N,L}:X^{b}_{N}(J)\to X^{b}_{N}(J)
, for
J=[-T,T]
, are the linear operators defined by

\mathcal{P}^{N,L}[\Psi](t)=-2i\chi(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{M}(u_{L},u_{L},\Psi)(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

(1.33)

Here the space
X^{b}_{N}(J)
is the projection of
X^{b}(J)
to its finite Fourier mode, i.e.
\langle k\rangle\leq N
.
Here
\langle k\rangle
is defined in (
1.44
).
The operator
\mathcal{P}^{N,L}
is known as the random averaging operator, which roughly acts as averaging over low-frequency objects.
Once the operator
\mathcal{P}^{N,L}
is properly controlled, as in Definition
3.1
, we can solve (
1.32
) and get

\Psi(t)=\mathcal{H}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)\Psi_{0}](t),

(1.34)

where

\mathcal{H}^{N,L}:=(1-\mathcal{P}^{N,L})^{-1}.

(1.35)

We denote
H_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}
the kernel of
\mathcal{H}^{N,L}
in the following sense, for a function
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
,

\mathcal{F}_{t}(\mathcal{H}^{N,L}[u])_{k}(\tau)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k^{\prime}}\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\widehat{u}_{k^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}.

Then, the solution (
1.34
) can be expressed as

\begin{split}\Psi(t,x)&=\mathcal{H}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)\Psi_{0}](t,x)\\
&=c\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k}\bigg[\sum_{k^{\prime}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\widehat{\chi}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\Big)(\Psi_{0})_{k^{\prime}}\bigg]e^{i(kx+t\tau)}d\tau.\end{split}

In what follows,
we set the initial data
\Psi_{0}=F_{N}
defined in
(
1.26
), and we denote
\psi_{N,L}
to be the corresponding solution, i.e.

\begin{split}\psi_{N,L}&(t,x)=\mathcal{H}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)F_{N}](t,x)\\
&=c\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k}\bigg[\sum_{\frac{N}{2}<\langle k^{\prime}\rangle\leq N}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\widehat{\chi}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\Big)\frac{g_{k^{\prime}}}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[k^{\prime}]\hskip-1.70717pt]^{\alpha/2}}\bigg]e^{i(kx+t\tau)}d\tau.\end{split}

(1.36)

From (
1.32
) and (
1.33
), we note that
\psi_{N,L}
defined in
(
1.36
) coincides with that in (
1.28
) when
L
is
chosen to be
L_{N}
in (
1.27
).
We define the operator

\mathfrak{h}^{N,L}:={\mathcal{H}}^{N,L}-{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L/2}.

(1.37)

By denoting by
\zeta^{N,L}:=\psi_{N,L}-\psi_{N,L/2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta^{N,L}&:=\psi_{N,L}-\psi_{N,L/2}={\mathfrak{h}}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)F_{N}].\end{split}

(1.38)

We also define the flow map version of the operator
\mathcal{H}^{N,L}
, denoted by
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L}(t)
and defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L}(t)[\phi](x):=\mathcal{H}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)\phi](t,x)=\sum_{k}e^{ikx}\sum_{k^{\prime}}\widetilde{{H}}^{N,L}_{kk^{\prime}}(t)\phi_{k^{\prime}},

(1.39)

where
\{\widetilde{{H}}^{N,L}_{kk^{\prime}}(t)\}_{kk^{\prime}}
is the kernel of the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L}(t)
. Then denote by

\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{N,L}(t):=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L}(t)-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,L/2}(t),

(1.40)

whose kernel is given by

h_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}(t)=\widetilde{{H}}^{N,L}_{kk^{\prime}}(t)-\widetilde{{H}}^{N,L/2}_{kk^{\prime}}(t).

(1.41)

One of the key observations is that
h_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}
and
\widetilde{H}_{kk^{\prime}}^{N,L}
are Borel functions of
(g_{k}(\omega))_{\langle k\rangle\leq L}
, and are thus independent
of the Gaussians
F_{N}
given by (
1.26
) and (
1.7
).
Such an independent structure enables one to use probabilistic tools to exploit the high-moment cancellation structure of
F_{N}
; see Proposition
2.24
.

1.4.3.
The solution structure

We note that, from (
1.29
), (
1.38
), and the fact that
\psi_{N,1/2}=F_{N}
,
we have the following formal expansion of
y_{N}
on
[-T,T]
.

\begin{split}y_{N}(t)&=\chi(t)\psi_{N,L_{N}}(t)+z_{N}(t)\\
&=\chi(t)F_{N}+\sum_{1\leq L\leq L_{N}}\zeta^{N,L}(t)+z_{N}(t)\\
&=\chi(t)F_{N}+\sum_{1\leq L\leq L_{N}}{\mathfrak{h}}^{N,L}[\chi(\cdot)F_{N}](t)+z_{N}(t)\\
&=\chi(t)F_{N}+\sum_{1\leq L\leq L_{N}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{N,L}[F_{N}](t)+z_{N}(t).\end{split}

(1.42)

We will construct
z_{N}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{N,L}
, for all
N\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
1\leq L\leq L_{N}
,
by an induction argument.
As a matter of fact, the random averaging operator
\mathfrak{h}^{N,L}
in (
1.42
) for
L\leq L_{N}
only depend on
(u_{L})_{L\ll N}
, and thus
(y_{L})_{L\ll N}
due to (
1.24
).
Then, from (
1.29
), we see that
(y_{L})_{L\ll N}
are again determined by
(z_{L^{\prime}})_{1\leq L^{\prime}\leq L}
and
(\mathfrak{h}^{L^{\prime},R^{\prime}})_{L^{\prime}\leq L,R^{\prime}<(L^{\prime})^{1-\delta}}
. In all, we see that
\mathfrak{h}^{N,L}
is determined by
(z_{L^{\prime}})_{L^{\prime}\ll N}
and
(\mathfrak{h}^{L^{\prime},R^{\prime}})_{L^{\prime},R^{\prime}\ll N}
. On the other hand, from (
1.30
), we see that
z_{N}
depends on
(z_{N^{\prime}})_{N^{\prime}\leq N}
and
(\mathfrak{h}^{N^{\prime},L})_{N^{\prime}\leq N,L<(N^{\prime})^{1-\delta}}
. See Definition
3.1
for further details.

Finally, from (
1.24
), (
1.42
) , and the fact that
u_{1/2}=F_{1/2}=0
, we arrive at:

u(t)=\chi(t)u_{0}^{\omega}+\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}}\sum_{1\leq L\leq L_{N}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{N,L}[F_{N}](t)+\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}}z_{N}(t),

(1.43)

provided the summations converge in a proper sense.
From (
1.43
), we see that the expected solution
u
in Theorem
1.2
has the following structure

u=\text{random linear term}+\text{random averaging term}+\text{smoother term}.

We shall construct the smoother term in
H^{\frac{1}{2}+}(\mathbb{T})
, much smoother than the random linear and random averaging terms, which lie in
H^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T})
, as
\alpha
is close to
1
.
The random averaging term bridges the random linear term and the smoother term.
On the one hand, it records quasilinear roughness from high-low interactions in
u
, thus guaranteeing that the “smoother term” has higher regularity. On the other hand, it preserves the independent structure of the initial data, such that we may apply probabilistic arguments.

Notation conventions.
Throughout the paper, we will denote
by
A\lesssim B
,
A\lesssim_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}B
and
A\ll B
any
estimate of the form
A\leq CB
,
A\leq C_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}B
and

A\leq cB
respectively, where
C>0
is an absolute constant,

C_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}>0
is a constant that only depends on parameters
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
, and
c>0
is a small constant. We also denote by
A\sim_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}B
if
A\lesssim_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}B
&
B\lesssim_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}}A
hold. We also use
a+
(and
a-
) to mean
a+\varepsilon
(and
a-\varepsilon
, respectively) for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
.
We denote by

\begin{cases}\langle k\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|k|^{2}};\\
[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]:=(1+|k|^{\alpha})^{1/\alpha}.\end{cases}

(1.44)

We note that
\langle k\rangle=[\hskip-1.70717pt[k]\hskip-1.70717pt]
for
\alpha=2
.
We denote
by
\bar{z}
the complex conjugate of
z
. We also use
(\mathcal{F}_{x}f)(k)
to denote the
Fourier coefficients of
f
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{x}f)(k)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)e^{-ikx}dx,

which will be abbreviated as
f_{k}
, i.e.
f_{k}=(\mathcal{F}_{x}f)(k)
. We may also abuse notations and write
f=(f_{k})
.
The Fourier transform is invertible such that

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{k}e^{ikx}.

Let
u=u(t,x)
be a space-time distribution. We denote
u_{k}(t):=\mathcal{F}_{x}(u(\cdot,t))(k)
to be the spatial Fourier coefficient of
u(\cdot,t)
. Given
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Fourier
coefficient
u_{k}
is still a function of time.
We use
\widehat{u_{k}}
to represent the temporal Fourier transform of
u_{k}
only, i.e.

\widehat{u_{k}}(\tau):=\mathcal{F}_{t}(u_{k})(\tau)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{k}(t)e^{-it\tau}dt.

We use
t
,
t^{\prime}
to
denote temporal variables and
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
,
\tau_{i}
to denote Fourier
variables of time.
When we work with space-time function spaces, we use short-hand notations such
as
C_{T}H_{x}^{s}=C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
.

Let
N_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
i=1,2,3
be dyadic numbers. We use
N_{\max}
,

N_{\med}
, and
N_{\min}
to denote the largest,
second largest, and smallest number among
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
, and
N_{3}
,
respectively.
Let
k_{i}
be the frequency associated with the dyadic number
N_{i}
in the sense that
\langle k_{i}\rangle\leq N_{i}
.
Please note that we only attach an upper bound to
\langle k_{i}\rangle
here.
If the object under consideration is of type (G)
1
1

1

See Subsection
3.2
for definitions of type (G), (C), and (D).
, then we may further restrict
\langle k_{i}\rangle
to the dyadic block
N_{i}/2<\langle k_{i}\rangle\leq N_{i}
, which is not necessarily true for type (C) and (D) objects.
Define
k_{\max}
to be the
k_{i}
such that
N_{i}=N_{\max}
.
When
N_{\max}=N_{\med}
, the roles of
k_{\max}
and
k_{\med}
are interchangeable in this context; i.e. if
N_{1}=N_{2}>N_{3}
, then we can designate either
k_{1}
or
k_{2}
as be
k_{\max}
and the other to be
k_{\med}
without changing other parts of the proof.
Similarly, we define
k_{\med}
and
k_{\min}
. We use
a\wedge b
and
a\vee b
to denote
\min(a,b)
and

\max(a,b)
, respectively. We denote by
\#S
or
|S|
the cardinal number of the set

S
.
We also need the sharp cutoff

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{B}(x)=\begin{cases}1,\quad&x\in B;\\
0,&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.45)

Let
X_{i}
for
i=1,2
be normed space equipped with norm
\|\cdot\|_{X_{i}}
.
Then we define

\|\cdot\|_{X_{1}\cap X_{2}}=\|\cdot\|_{X_{1}}+\|\cdot\|_{X_{2}}.

Similarly, we can define the intersection of more than two normed spaces.

For any
N
, we denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\leq N}
the

\sigma
-algebra generated by the random variables
(g_{k})_{\langle k\rangle\leq N}
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section

2
, we recall some basic definitions and tools and we also
prove some crucial tensor estimates. In Section
3
, we prove the
main theorem. In Sections
4
and
5
, we prove Proposition

3.3
. In Section
6
, we discuss the
sharpness of the condition
\alpha>1
.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we collect some results that will be used throughout the paper.

2.1.
Norms

Let
A
be a finite index set.
We denote by
k_{A}=\{k_{j};j\in A\}\in\mathbb{Z}^{A}
.
A tensor
H=H_{k_{A}}
is a function
\mathbb{Z}^{A}\to\mathbb{C}
with variables
k_{A}
. In what follows, we assume
H_{k_{A}}\in\ell^{2}_{k_{A}}
, i.e.

\|H\|_{\ell^{2}_{A}}:=\|H_{k_{A}}\|_{k_{A}}=\bigg(\sum_{k_{A}}|H_{k_{A}}|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty.

The support of
H
is the set of
k_{A}
such that
H_{k_{A}}\neq 0
.
If
(B,C)
is a partition of
A
, namely
B\cap C=\emptyset
and
B\cup C=A
, we define the tensor norm
\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}
such that

\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}^{2}=\sup\bigg\{\sum_{k_{C}}\Big|\sum_{k_{B}}H_{k_{A}}z_{k_{B}}\Big|^{2};\sum_{k_{B}}|z_{k_{B}}|^{2}=1\bigg\}.

(2.1)

Let us remark that

\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}=\|H\|_{k_{C}\to k_{B}}\leq\|H\|_{k_{A}}.

(2.2)

Let
f\in\ell^{2}_{B}
be a function
f:\mathbb{Z}^{B}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\|f({k_{B}})\|_{\ell^{2}_{B}}<\infty
.
We may use
H
to define an operator, still denoted by
H:\ell^{2}_{k_{B}}\to\ell^{2}_{k_{C}}
, given by

H[f](k_{C})=\sum_{k_{B}}H_{k_{A}}f({k_{B}}).

Then the tensor norm
\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}
defined in (
2.1
) can be recast as an operator norm.

\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}=\|H\|_{\ell_{k_{B}}^{2}\to\ell_{k_{C}}^{2}}:=\sup_{\|f\|_{\ell^{2}_{k_{B}}}=1}\|H[f]\|_{\ell^{2}_{k_{C}}}.

We remark that if
C=\emptyset
, then
B=A
and
\|H\|_{k_{B}\to k_{C}}=\|H\|_{k_{A}}
.

For a space-time function
u\in C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
, we have

u(t,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k}(t)e^{ikx}.

We define the Fourier restriction norm

\|u\|_{X^{b}}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\|\widehat{u_{k}}(\tau)\|_{k}^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\sum_{k}|\widehat{u_{k}}(\tau)|^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

For a time dependent operator
\mathcal{H}(t)
given by its kernel
\mathcal{H}(t)=\{H_{kk^{\prime}}(t)\}_{kk^{\prime}}
, i.e.

\mathcal{H}(t)[f]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{kk^{\prime}}(t)f_{k^{\prime}}\Big)e^{ikx},

we define the operator norm

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathcal{H}\|_{Y^{b}}&:=\big\|\langle\tau\rangle^{b}\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau)\big\|_{\ell_{k}^{2}L_{\tau}^{2}\to\ell_{k^{\prime}}^{2}}\\
&=\sup\bigg\{\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\sum_{k}\Big|\sum_{k^{\prime}}\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau)f_{k^{\prime}}\Big|^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}};\,\,\sum_{k^{\prime}}|f_{k^{\prime}}|^{2}=1\bigg\}\\
&=\sup_{\|f\|_{L^{2}}=1}\big\|\mathcal{H}(t)[f]\big\|_{X^{b}},\end{split}

(2.3)

and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the operator
\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}\|_{Z^{b}}:=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\|\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau)\|_{kk^{\prime}}^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}}\big|\widehat{H_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau)\big|^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.4)

Now we consider an operator
\mathcal{P}
over space-time functions given by

\mathcal{P}[u](t,x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg(\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\widehat{u}_{k^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\bigg)e^{i(t\tau+kx)}d\tau,

where
\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
is the double temporal Fourier transform given by

\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P_{kk^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})e^{-i(t\tau+t^{\prime}\tau^{\prime})}dtdt^{\prime}.

We define the operator norm of
\mathcal{P}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{Y^{b,b^{\prime}}}&:=\big\|\langle\tau\rangle^{b}\langle\tau^{\prime}\rangle^{-b^{\prime}}\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\big\|_{\ell_{k}^{2}L_{\tau}^{2}\to\ell_{k^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\tau^{\prime}}^{2}}\\
&=\sup_{\|u\|_{X^{b^{\prime}}}=1}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\sum_{k}\bigg|\sum_{k^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\widehat{u}_{k^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\bigg|^{2}d\tau\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&=\sup_{\|u\|_{X^{b^{\prime}}}=1}\big\|\mathcal{P}[u]\big\|_{X^{b}},\end{split}

(2.5)

and the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the operator
\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{Z^{b,b^{\prime}}}&=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\tau\rangle^{2b}\langle\tau^{\prime}\rangle^{-2b^{\prime}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}}\big|\widehat{P_{kk^{\prime}}}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\big|^{2}d\tau d\tau^{\prime}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(2.6)

For any finite interval
I
, define the corresponding localized norms

\|u\|_{X^{b}(I)}:=\inf\left\{\|v\|_{X^{b}};v=u\text{ on }I\right\}

and similarly define
Y^{b,b^{\prime}}(I)
and
Z^{b,b^{\prime}}(I)
. By abusing
notations, we will call the above
v
an extension of
u
,
and still denotes
v
by
u
. We will use
X^{b}
to denote
X^{b}(I)
for simplicity unless otherwise specified.

To conclude this subsection, we record some basic estimates from
[
17
,
19
]
. Define the original and truncated integral
operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}v(t)=\int^{t}_{0}v(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},\quad\mathcal{I}_{\eta}v(t)=\eta(t)\int^{t}_{0}\eta(t^{\prime})v(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},

(2.7)

where
\eta(t)
is a smooth cutoff function, such that
\eta(t)=1
for
|t|\leq 1
; and
\eta(t)=0
for
|t|\geq 2
.
By comparing equations (
1.13
) with (
1.21
),
we observe that the Duhamel operator from (
1.13
) roughly reduces to the integral operator
\mathcal{I}
defined in (
2.7
) under the transformation
u(t)\to e^{it{\rm D}_{x}^{\alpha}}u(t)
.
Therefore, the operators
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\eta}
are also referred to as Duhamel operator and truncated Duhamel operator, respectively.
See
[
19
, Section 4.1]
for further details.
We have the following kernel estimates. See
[
17
, Lemma 3.1]
and
[
19
, Lemma 4.1]
for a proof.

Lemma 2.1
.

We have the formula

\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\eta}v}(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{K}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})\hat{v}(\lambda^{\prime})d\lambda^{\prime},

(2.8)

where the kernel
\mathcal{K}
satisfies the following pointwise estimates

|\mathcal{K}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})|+|\partial_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})|\lesssim\bigg(\frac{1}{\langle\lambda\rangle^{3}}+\frac{1}{\langle\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rangle^{3}}\bigg)\frac{1}{\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle}\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle\lambda\rangle\langle\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\rangle}.

(2.9)

We also need the following short-time bound on Fourier restriction norm from
[
18
, Proposition 2.7]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

(Short time bounds).
Let
\eta
be any Schwartz
function. Recall that
\eta_{T}(t)=\eta(T^{-1}t)
for
T\ll 1
. Then for any
u\in X^{b_{1}}
, we have

\|\eta_{T}\cdot u\|_{X^{b}}\lesssim T^{b_{1}-b}\|u\|_{X^{b_{1}}},

provided either
0<b\leq b_{1}<1/2
, or
u_{k}(0)=0
and
1/2<b\leq b_{1}<1
.

2.2.
Counting estimates

Counting estimates are crucial in our analysis.
In this subsection,
we shall show some almost sharp counting estimates of solutions to some fractional Diophantine equations.
To be more precise,
given dyadic numbers
N
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
,
N_{3}>0
and some fixed number
m\in\mathbb{R}
, define the set

S=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4},\quad k_{2}\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\},\\
&k=k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3},\\
&|{k_{1}}|^{\alpha}-|{k_{2}}|^{\alpha}+|{k_{3}}|^{\alpha}-|{k}|^{\alpha}=m+O(1),\\
&|k|\leq N,\,\,|k_{j}|\leq N_{j}\,\textup{ for }j\in\{1,2,3\},\end{aligned}\right.

(2.10)

which is a subset of the hyperplane
k=k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
.
Denote by
S_{k}
the set of
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that
(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in S
when
k
is fixed.
It is easy to see that

S_{k}\subset\Gamma(k),

where
\Gamma(k)
is the hyperplane of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
defined in (
1.15
). Similarly, we can define
S_{k_{1}}
,
S_{kk_{1}}
,
S_{kk_{1}k_{2}}
, and etc.
For a set
A
, we use
|A|
or
\#A
to denote its cardinality. If
A
is a finite set, then
|A|
denotes the number of elements of
A
.
The main purpose of this subsection is to count the cardinality of these sets defined above.

We start with a basic counting estimate from
[
42
, Lemma 2.6]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
I
, J be two intervals and
\phi
be a
real-valued
C^{1}
function defined on
I
. Then, we have

\#\{k\in I\cap\mathbb{Z};\phi(k)\in J\}\lesssim 1+\frac{|J|}{\operatorname{inf}\limits_{\xi\in I}|\phi^{\prime}(\xi)|}.

In this paper, we will focus on a special function
\phi
. Let

\displaystyle\phi_{b,\pm}(x)=|x|^{\alpha}\pm|x-b|^{\alpha},

(2.11)

where
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, we see that
\phi_{b,\pm}\in C^{1}
for
\alpha>1
, and we have

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{b,\pm}(x)=\alpha\big(\operatorname{sgn}(x)|x|^{\alpha-1}\pm\operatorname{sgn}(x-b)|x-b|^{\alpha-1}\big).

(2.12)

Then, we have the following elementary estimates.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\phi_{b,\pm}
be as in (
2.11
).

(i)

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
Then, we have

|\phi^{\prime}_{b,-}(x)|\gtrsim_{\alpha}\min(|b||x|^{\alpha-2},|b|^{\alpha-1})

(2.13)

provided
x\neq 0
.

(ii)

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
|b|\geq 1
. Then, we have

|\phi_{b,+}^{\prime}(x)|\gtrsim_{\alpha}|b|^{\alpha-1}

(2.14)

for
|2x-b|\gtrsim|b|
.
For
|2x-b|\ll|b|
, if we further assume that
|2x-b|\gtrsim|b|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, then we have

|\phi_{b,+}^{\prime}(x)|\gtrsim_{\alpha}|b|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}.

(2.15)

Proof.

(i) We first consider
b<0
, for which we use (
2.12
) to get

\phi^{\prime}_{b,-}(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha(-|x|^{\alpha-1}+|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(-\infty,b];\\
\alpha(-|x|^{\alpha-1}-|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(b,0);\\
\alpha(|x|^{\alpha-1}-|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(0,+\infty).\end{cases}

When
x\in(b,0)
, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}_{b,-}(x)|=\alpha(|x|^{\alpha-1}+|x-b|^{\alpha-1})\gtrsim_{\alpha}|b|^{\alpha-1},

due to the fact that
\alpha>1
, which is sufficient for (
2.13
).
We turn to the case when
x\in(-\infty,b]\cup(0,+\infty)
, where we have that
x
and
x-b
have the same sign.
Therefore, by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\phi^{\prime}_{b,-}(x)|=\alpha(\alpha-1)\bigg|\int_{b}^{0}|x-b+s|^{\alpha-2}ds\bigg|\\
&\quad\gtrsim_{\alpha}|b|\cdot\min(|x|^{\alpha-2},|x-b|^{\alpha-2}),\end{split}

(2.16)

where we used the fact that
\alpha-2<0
. If
x\in(-\infty,b]
, then
|x-b|\leq|x|
and thus
\min(|x|^{\alpha-2},|x-b|^{\alpha-2})=|x|^{\alpha-2}
, which, together with (
2.16
), gives (
2.13
). In the following, let us assume that
x\in(0,\infty)
. Then we have
|x-b|\geq|x|
. If we further assume that
|x-b|\sim|x|
, then
\min(|x|^{\alpha-2},|x-b|^{\alpha-2})\gtrsim|x|^{\alpha-2}
, which is again sufficient for (
2.13
).
It remains to consider the case
|x-b|\gg|x|
, which implies
|x|\ll|b|
and thus
|x-b|\sim|b|
. Therefore, we have
\min(|x|^{\alpha-2},|x-b|^{\alpha-2})=|x-b|^{\alpha-2}\sim|b|^{\alpha-2}
, which, together with (
2.16
), gives (
2.13
) again.
Thus we finish the proof of (
2.13
) for
b<0
. The proof for the case when
b>0
is similar and therefore omitted.

(ii) We only consider the case
b<0
, as the proof for the case
b>0
is similar.
From (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{b,+}(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha(-|x|^{\alpha-1}-|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(-\infty,b];\\
\alpha(-|x|^{\alpha-1}+|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(b,0);\\
\alpha(|x|^{\alpha-1}+|x-b|^{\alpha-1}),&x\in(0,+\infty).\end{cases}

(2.17)

When
x\in(-\infty,b]\cup(0,\infty)
, from (
2.17
) we have

|\phi_{b,+}^{\prime}(x)|=\alpha||x|^{\alpha-1}+|b-x|^{\alpha-1}|\gtrsim_{\alpha}|b|^{\alpha-1},

since
\alpha>1
.
From now on, we assume
x\in(b,0]
.
From (
2.17
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\phi_{b,+}^{\prime}(x)|&=\alpha\big||x|^{\alpha-1}-|x-b|^{\alpha-1}\big|\\
&={\alpha}{(\alpha-1)}\bigg|\int_{x}^{0}t^{\alpha-2}dt-\int_{0}^{x-b}t^{\alpha-2}dt\bigg|\\
&={\alpha}{(\alpha-1)}\bigg|\int_{-x}^{x-b}t^{\alpha-2}dt\bigg|\\
&\geq{\alpha}{(\alpha-1)}|2x-b||b|^{\alpha-2},\end{split}

(2.18)

where we used the facts that
|x|,|x-b|\leq|b|
.
It is easy to see that (
2.18
) implies (
2.14
), provided
|2x-b|\gtrsim|b|
.
On the other hand, if
|2x-b|\gtrsim|b|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\alpha}{(\alpha-1)}|2x-b||b|^{\alpha-2}\gtrsim|b|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1},

(2.19)

which, together with (
2.18
), gives (
2.15
).
Thus we finish the proof of (
2.15
).
∎

Now we are ready to show our main counting estimates.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
1\leq N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\leq N
.
Then we have the following counting estimates

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{1}}|&\lesssim(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k\rangle^{-1}+1;\\
|S_{kk_{3}}|&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\langle k-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1;\\
|S_{k_{1}k_{2}}|&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+1;\\
|S_{k_{2}k_{3}}|&\lesssim N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{2}-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1,\end{split}

(2.20)

where
\langle k\rangle=(1+|k|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
is given in (
1.44
).

Proof.

We start with the bound for
|S_{kk_{1}}|
.
We first note that
k\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\}
is equivalent to
k_{2}\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\}
on the hyperplane
k=k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}
.
Moreover, on this hyperplane, for fixed
(k,k_{1})
(such that
|k|\leq N
,
|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}
, and
k\neq k_{1}
), once we further fix
k_{3}
, then
k_{2}
is uniquely determined. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{1}}|&=|\{k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{3}\neq k,|k_{3}|\leq N_{3},|k_{1}+k_{3}-k|\leq N_{2},\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt|k_{3}|^{\alpha}-|k_{1}+k_{3}-k|^{\alpha}=|k|^{\alpha}-|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-m+O(1)\}|\\
&=|\{k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{3}\neq k,|k_{3}|\leq N_{3},|k_{1}+k_{3}-k|\leq N_{2},\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\phi_{k-k_{1},-}(k_{3})=|k|^{\alpha}-|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-m+O(1)\}|,\end{split}

where
\phi_{k-k_{1},-}
is given in (
2.11
) with
b=k-k_{1}
.
We now apply Lemma
2.3
and then Lemma
2.4
to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{1}}|&\lesssim 1+\frac{1}{\inf_{|k_{3}|\lesssim N_{3}}|\phi^{\prime}_{k-k_{1},-}(k_{3})|}\\
&\lesssim 1+\big(\min(\inf_{|k_{3}|\leq N_{3}}|k-k_{1}||k_{3}|^{\alpha-2},|k-k_{1}|^{\alpha-1})\big)^{-1}\\
&\lesssim 1+\langle k-k_{1}\rangle^{-1}N_{3}^{2-\alpha},\end{split}

(2.21)

where
k\neq k_{1}
and
\alpha\in(1,2)
.

By switching
k_{2}
and
k_{3}
in the above argument, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{1}}|&=|\{k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{2}\neq k_{1},|k_{2}|\leq N_{2},|k_{1}-k_{2}-k|\leq N_{3},\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt|k_{2}|^{\alpha}-|k_{1}-k_{2}-k|^{\alpha}=|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-|k|^{\alpha}+m+O(1)\}|\\
&=|\{k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{2}\neq k_{1},|k_{2}|\leq N_{2},|k_{1}-k_{2}-k|\leq N_{3},\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\phi_{k_{1}-k,-}(k_{2})=|k_{1}|^{\alpha}-|k|^{\alpha}+m+O(1)\}|.\end{split}

Then by the same argument as in (
2.21
), we get

\displaystyle|S_{kk_{1}}|\lesssim 1+\langle k-k_{1}\rangle^{-1}N_{2}^{2-\alpha}.

(2.22)

Thus we finish the proof of (
2.20
) for the bound
S_{kk_{1}}
by combining (
2.21
) and (
2.22
).

The proof for the rest of (
2.20
) is similar; thus, we omit their details.
∎

We point out that the decay
\langle k-k_{1}\rangle^{-1}
in (
2.20
) plays a crucial role in our later analysis.
We have the following estimates as a consequence of Lemma
2.5
.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
1\leq N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\leq N
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{k}|&\lesssim\min((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3});\\
|S_{k_{1}}|&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{2}+N_{2};\\
|S_{k_{2}}|&\lesssim\min(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3});\\
|S_{k_{3}}|&\lesssim N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{2}+N_{2}.\end{split}

(2.23)

Proof.

We start with the bound for
|S_{k}|
. We first note that

\displaystyle|S_{k}|\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}}|S_{kk_{1}}|,{\sum_{|k_{3}|\leq N_{3}}}|S_{kk_{3}}|\bigg).

(2.24)

By using Lemma
2.5
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}}|S_{kk_{1}}|&\lesssim(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}}\langle k_{1}-k\rangle^{-1}+\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}}1\\
&\lesssim(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},\end{split}

(2.25)

and similarly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{|k_{3}|\leq N_{3}}|S_{kk_{3}}|&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\sum_{|k_{3}|\leq N_{3}}\langle k-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{3}}1\\
&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3}.\end{split}

(2.26)

Therefore, the first bound in (
2.23
) follows from (
2.24
), (
2.25
), and (
2.26
).

By a similar argument, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{k_{1}}|&\lesssim\sum_{|k_{2}|\leq N_{2}}|S_{k_{1}k_{2}}|\\
&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\sum_{|k_{2}|\leq N_{2}}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+\sum_{|k_{2}|\leq N_{2}}1\\
&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{2}+N_{2}.\end{split}

Thus, we finish the proof for the second estimate of (
2.23
).

The proof for the rest of (
2.23
) can be handled similarly. Thus, we omit them.
∎

For the countings of
|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}|
and
|S_{kk_{2}}|
, the argument in Lemma
2.5
is not sufficient.
We need the following further decomposition.

\begin{cases}S^{\rm bad}_{k_{1}k_{3}}=\left\{(k,k_{2})\in S_{k_{1}k_{3}};|2k-(k_{1}+k_{3})|\ll|k_{1}+k_{3}|\right\};\\
S^{\rm good}_{k_{1}k_{3}}=\left\{(k,k_{2})\in S_{k_{1}k_{3}};|2k-(k_{1}+k_{3})|\gtrsim|k_{1}+k_{3}|\right\};\\
S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}=\left\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\ll|k+k_{2}|\right\};\\
S^{\rm good}_{kk_{2}}=\left\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\gtrsim|k+k_{2}|\right\}.\end{cases}

(2.27)

With these notations, we have the following improved counting estimates.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
Then we have the following counting estimates

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}|,|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}^{\rm good}|&\lesssim 1;\\
|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}|&\lesssim|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+1;\\
|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}^{\rm bad}|&\lesssim|k_{1}+k_{3}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+1.\end{split}

(2.28)

Proof.

We start with the estimate of
|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}|
with
k+k_{2}\neq 0
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma
2.5
, we may recast the set
S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}|&=|S_{kk_{2}}\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\gtrsim|k+k_{2}|\}|\\
&=|\{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{1}\neq k,|k_{1}|\leq N_{1},|k_{1}-k_{2}-k|\leq N_{3},\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt\phi_{k+k_{2},+}(k_{1})=|k|^{\alpha}+|k_{2}|^{\alpha}-m+O(1)\}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\gtrsim|k+k_{2}|\}|,\end{split}

where
\phi_{k+k_{2},+}
is given in (
2.11
) with
b=k+k_{2}
.
Thus by using Lemma
2.3
and then (
2.14
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}|\lesssim 1+|k+k_{2}|^{1-\alpha}\lesssim 1,\end{split}

since
k+k_{2}\neq 0
.
Thus, we finish the proof of the bound for
|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm good}|
.

Now we turn to
|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}|
.
We distinguish two cases.
We first consider the case when
|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\leq|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
, for which we have the crude estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}&\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\leq|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\}|\\
&\leq|\{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\leq|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\}|\\
&\lesssim|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+1,\end{split}

(2.29)

which is sufficient for our purpose. From now on, we assume
|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|>|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
.
Similar to the above, we may rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}&\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|>|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\}|\\
&=|S_{kk_{2}}\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\leq|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\ll|k+k_{2}|\}|\\
&=|\{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z};k_{1}\neq k,|k_{1}|\leq N_{1},|k_{1}-k_{2}-k|\leq N_{3},\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt\phi_{k+k_{2},+}(k_{1})=|k|^{\alpha}+|k_{2}|^{\alpha}-m+O(1)\}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\leq|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\ll|k+k_{2}|\}|.\end{split}

Then we may apply Lemma
2.3
, together with (
2.15
), to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}&\cap\{(k_{1},k_{3})\in S_{kk_{2}};|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\leq|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\}|\\
&\lesssim 1+|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\end{split}

(2.30)

which is again sufficient for our purpose. By collecting (
2.29
) and (
2.30
), we proved the estimate for
|S_{kk_{2}}^{\rm bad}|
.

The proof for the rest of (
2.28
) is similar and thus omitted.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

The following bounds hold.

|S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}|\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{3})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}};\qquad|S^{\rm bad}_{k_{1}k_{3}}|\lesssim(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}},

where
S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}
and
S^{\rm bad}_{k_{1}k_{3}}
are defined in (
2.27
).

Proof.

We only consider the estimate for
|S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}|
, as the proof for the bound of
|S^{\rm bad}_{k_{1}k_{3}}|
is similar. We first note that for
(k_{1},k_{3})\in S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}
, we have

|2k_{3}-(k+k_{2})|=|2k_{1}-(k+k_{2})|\ll|k+k_{2}|,

which implies that

\displaystyle|k_{1}|\sim|k+k_{2}|\sim|k_{3}|.

(2.31)

Then recall that
|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}
and
|k_{3}|\leq N_{3}
, which together with Lemma
2.7
and (
2.31
), implies

|S^{\rm bad}_{kk_{2}}|\lesssim|k+k_{2}|^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+1\lesssim\min(N_{1}^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}},N_{3}^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}).

Thus, we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

As a consequence of Lemma
2.7
and Corollary
2.8
, we have shown that

\displaystyle|S_{kk_{2}}|\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{3})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\qquad|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}|\lesssim(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

With this improvement, we may deduce further upper bounds for
|S_{k_{1}}|
and
|S_{k_{3}}|
as follows

\displaystyle|S_{k_{1}}|\lesssim N_{3}(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}},\qquad|S_{k_{3}}|\lesssim N_{1}(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.

(2.32)

Finally, we are ready to estimate the size of
|S|
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
S
be given in (
2.10
) with
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
1\leq N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\leq N
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|S|&\lesssim\min\big(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})\log(N_{1}\vee N_{2})+N_{1}N_{2},\\
&\hskip 42.67912ptN_{1}^{2-\alpha}(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})\log(N_{2}\vee N_{3})+N_{2}N_{3}\big).\end{split}

(2.33)

Proof.

We first observe that

\displaystyle|S|\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}|S_{k_{1}k_{2}}|,\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}}|S_{k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg).

(2.34)

We shall estimate the right-hand side of (
2.34
) term by term.
From (
2.20
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}&|S_{k_{1}k_{2}}|\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}1\\
&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\sum_{|k_{1}|\leq N_{1}}\sum_{|k_{2}|\leq N_{2}}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+N_{1}N_{2}\\
&\lesssim N_{3}^{2-\alpha}(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})\log(N_{1}\vee N_{2})+N_{1}N_{2}.\end{split}

(2.35)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}}|S_{k_{2}k_{3}}|\lesssim N_{1}^{2-\alpha}(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})\log(N_{2}\vee N_{3})+N_{2}N_{3}.

(2.36)

Then (
2.33
) follows from (
2.35
) and (
2.36
).
∎

Another structure that we shall exploit is the so-called
\Gamma
-condition.
To be more precise,
if there exists a positive number
\Gamma
such that

|k_{\max}|\leq\Gamma<|k|,

where
k_{\max}
is the frequency corresponding to
N_{\max}
,
then we call that
S
given in (
2.10
) satisfies the
\Gamma
-condition. To simplify the notation, let

\displaystyle B_{\Gamma}=\{(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in S;|k_{\max}|\leq\Gamma<|k|\}.

(2.37)

From the fact that
k=k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}
, we see that

\begin{cases}\Gamma\leq|k|\leq\Gamma+2N_{\med};\\
\Gamma-2N_{\med}\leq|k_{\max}|\leq\Gamma.\end{cases}

(2.38)

Thus, both
|k|
and
|k_{\max}|
locate in an interval of length
2N_{\med}
.

With the
\Gamma
-condition, we can improve the previous counting estimates.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
S
be given in (
2.10
) with
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
B_{\Gamma}
be as in (
2.37
), and
1\leq N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\leq N
. Then, we have

\displaystyle|{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S|

\displaystyle\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med};

(2.39)

\displaystyle|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k}|,|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{i}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim N_{\med},\,\,\text{ for }\,\,i=1,2,3;

(2.40)

\displaystyle|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{\min}k_{\med}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim N_{\med}.

(2.41)

Here
({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k}
is the set of
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that
(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S
when
k
is fixed. The definitions of
({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{i}}
and
({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{\min}k_{\med}}
are similar.

Proof.

The key observation is that under the
\Gamma
-condition (
2.38
), both
|k|
and
|k_{\max}|
are confined in some intervals of size
2N_{\med}
.
We first prove (
2.39
).
Recall that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S|\\
&\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k,k_{1}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{kk_{1}}|,\sum_{k,k_{3}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{kk_{3}}|,\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{1}k_{2}}|,\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg)\\
&\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k,k_{1}}\big((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k\rangle^{-1}+1\big),\sum_{k,k_{3}}\big((N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\langle k-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1\big),\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\big(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+1\big),\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}}\big(N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{2}-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\bigg),\end{split}

(2.42)

where the summations are under (
2.38
).
We then distinguish three cases.
If
N_{1}=N_{\min}
, then from (
2.38
) and (
2.42
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S|&\lesssim\sum_{k,k_{1}}\big((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k_{1}}\big[(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\med}\big]\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\min}N_{\med}\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med},\end{split}

(2.43)

where we used the
\Gamma
-condition (
2.38
) in the first step when summing over
k
. Thus we finish the proof for the case
N_{1}=N_{\min}
.
If
N_{3}=N_{\min}
, the argument is similar and thus omitted.
In the following, we assume that
N_{2}=N_{\min}
. If we further assume that
N_{3}=N_{\med}
, then similar computation as in (
2.43
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S|&\lesssim\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\big(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k_{2}}\big(N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\med}\big)\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\min}N_{\med}\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med}.\end{split}

It remains to consider the case when
N_{2}=N_{\min}
and
N_{1}=N_{\med}
.
Then, from (
2.38
) and (
2.42
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{B_{\Gamma}}\cap S|&\lesssim\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}}\big(N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{2}-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k_{2}}(N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\med})\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{\med}+N_{\min}N_{\med}\\
&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med}.\end{split}

Thus we finish the proof.

Now we consider (
2.40
). We first note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k}|\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{kk_{1}}|,\sum_{k_{3}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{kk_{3}}|\bigg)\\
&\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}}\big((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k\rangle^{-1}+1\big),\sum_{k_{3}}\big((N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\langle k-k_{3}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\bigg)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k_{\med}}\big(N_{\med}^{2-\alpha}\langle k-k_{\med}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\lesssim N_{\med}^{2-\alpha+\varepsilon}+N_{\med}\lesssim N_{\med}.\end{split}

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{1}}|&\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k_{2}}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{k_{1}k_{2}}|,\,\sum_{k}|({B_{\Gamma}}\cap S)_{kk_{1}}|\bigg)\\
&\lesssim\min\bigg(\sum_{k_{2}}\big(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\langle k_{1}-k_{2}\rangle^{-1}+1\big),\,\sum_{k}\big(N_{2}^{2-\alpha}\langle k-k_{1}\rangle^{-1}+1\big)\bigg)\\
&\lesssim N_{\med},\end{split}

where in the last step, we used (
2.38
), which suggests the summation over
k_{2}
is at most over an interval of size
N_{\med}
.
The rest of (
2.40
) follows similarly.

Finally, we turn to (
2.41
).
We note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|(B_{\Gamma}\cap S)_{k_{\min}k_{\med}}|&=|S_{k_{\min}k_{\med}}\cap\{\eqref{Eqn:gamma}\}|\\
&\leq|\{k_{\max}\textup{ range in an interval of size at most }2N_{\med}\}|\\
&\leq 2N_{\med}.\end{split}

Thus, we finish the proof of (
2.41
).
∎

2.3.
Tensor norm estimates

This subsection will introduce the base tensor operator and then discuss its operator norm estimates.
Given
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
, and dyadic numbers
1\leq N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\leq N
,
we define the base tensor
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
as

\displaystyle{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}={\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=\mathbf{1}_{S}(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(2.44)

where
\mathbf{1}_{S}
is the indicator function defined in (
1.45
), and
S
is the subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
given in (
2.10
).
Here, with a slight abuse of notation, we will not distinguish the tensor operator
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
and its kernel
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}
.
From (
2.10
) we see that

\begin{split}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}&=\mathbf{1}_{k-k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}=0}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}\notin\{k_{1},k_{3}\}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{|k|^{\alpha}-|k_{1}|^{\alpha}+|k_{2}|^{\alpha}-|k_{3}|^{\alpha}=m+O(1)\}}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXXX}\times\mathbf{1}_{|k|\leq N}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{1}_{|k_{i}|\leq N_{i}}.\end{split}

Recall the operator norms defined in (
2.1
). For example,

\displaystyle\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=\sum_{k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\big|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\big|^{2}=|S|,

(2.45)

or

\begin{split}&\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{k_{2}k_{3}\to kk_{1}}=\sup\bigg\{\sum_{k,k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big|\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}z_{k_{2}k_{3}}\Big|^{2};\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|z_{k_{2}k_{3}}|^{2}=1\bigg\}.\end{split}

Now we are ready to state the estimates for tensor norms, which are the main tools of our later analysis.
We start with the Hilbert–Schmidt norm estimate of the base tensor operator
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
, which is a consequence of Lemma
2.10
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
be the base tensor defined in (
2.44
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim\min\big(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})\log(N_{1}\vee N_{2})+N_{1}N_{2},\\
&\hskip 42.67912ptN_{1}^{2-\alpha}(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})\log(N_{2}\vee N_{3})+N_{2}N_{3}).\end{split}

(2.46)

Proof.

The estimate (
2.46
) is a consequence of (
2.45
) and Lemma
2.10
.
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
be the base tensor defined in (
2.44
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}\to k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-{\alpha}}N_{1}^{2-\alpha},\\
\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{3}\to k_{1}k_{2}}^{2}&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}N_{3}^{2-\alpha},\\
\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{2}\to k_{1}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{3})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}.\end{split}

(2.47)

Proof.

We start with the first estimate of (
2.47
).
By the Schur’s test, we have

\begin{split}\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{kk_{1}\to k_{2}k_{3}}&\lesssim\bigg(\sup_{k,k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg)\times\bigg(\sup_{k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k,k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg).\end{split}

which gives the first estimate of (
2.47
) by using Lemma
2.5
.
Same argument as above works for the second estimate of (
2.47
).
For the third estimate, we use Remark
2.9
instead of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
be the base tensor defined in (
2.44
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k\to k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim\min((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3});\\
\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{1}\to kk_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim\min(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{2}+N_{2},N_{3}(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}});\\
\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{2}\to kk_{1}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim\min(N_{3}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3});\\
\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{3}\to kk_{1}k_{2}}^{2}&\lesssim\min(N_{1}^{2-\alpha}\log N_{2}+N_{2},N_{1}(N\wedge N_{2})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}).\\
\end{split}

(2.48)

Proof.

Similar to in the proof of Lemma
2.13
, the estimates of (
2.48
) are consequences of (
2.23
). We only show how to get the first estimate, as others follow similarly
2
2

2

For the second and fourth estimates, we may need to use (
2.32
) as well.
.

By the Schur’s test, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{k\to k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}&\lesssim\bigg(\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg)\times\bigg(\sup_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\bigg).\end{split}

(2.49)

We note that once
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
are fixed, then
k
is uniquely determined provided
(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in S
, where
S
is given in (
2.10
). Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\leq 1.

(2.50)

On the other hand, for fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, from (
2.44
) and (
2.23
), we note

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}&|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|=|S_{k}|\\
&\lesssim\min((N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{1}+N_{1},(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{2-\alpha}\log N_{3}+N_{3}).\end{split}

(2.51)

Then, the first estimate of (
2.48
) follows from (
2.49
), (
2.50
), and (
2.51
).
∎

We will need some estimates of the base tensor
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
subject to further restrictions,
for which we expect better estimates.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
be the base tensor defined in (
2.44
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{1}N_{3};\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{2}\to k_{1}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim(N_{1}\wedge N_{3})^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}};\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{1}\to kk_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{3};\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{3}\to kk_{1}k_{2}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{1}.\end{split}

(2.52)

Proof.

We start with the first bound in (
2.48
).
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&=\sum_{k_{1},k_{3}}\sum_{k,k_{2}}|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k_{1},k_{3}}|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}\cap\{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|\}|.\end{split}

(2.53)

We observe that under the condition
|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|
, we have

|2k-(k_{1}+k_{3})|=|2k_{2}-(k_{1}+k_{3})|\geq 2|k_{2}|-|k_{1}+k_{3}|>|k_{2}|>|k_{1}+k_{3}|,

which, together with (
2.27
), implies that

\displaystyle S_{k_{1}k_{3}}\cap\{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|\}\subset S_{k_{1}k_{3}}^{\rm good}.

(2.54)

Thus, from (
2.49
), (
2.50
), (
2.53
), (
2.54
), and Lemma
2.7
, we get

\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}\leq\sum_{k_{1},k_{3}}|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}^{\rm good}|\lesssim N_{1}N_{3},

which finishes the proof of the first estimate of (
2.48
).

We turn to the estimate of
\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{2}\to k_{1}k_{3}}
.
By a similar Schur’s test argument as in (
2.49
), we have

\begin{split}\|&\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{kk_{2}\to k_{1}k_{3}}\\
&\lesssim\Big(\sup_{k,k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\Big)\times\Big(\sup_{k_{1},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k,k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\Big)\\
&=\Big(\sup_{k,k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}|S_{kk_{2}}|\Big)\cdot\Big(\sup_{k_{1},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|S^{\rm good}_{k_{1}k_{3}}|\Big),\end{split}

which, together with Lemma
2.7
, gives the desired bound.

For the third estimate
\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{1}\to kk_{2}k_{3}}
,
we proceed similarly as the above to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|^{2}_{k_{1}\to kk_{2}k_{3}}&\lesssim\sup_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k,k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|\mathbf{1}_{|k_{1}+k_{3}|<|k_{2}|}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}|\\
&\lesssim\sup_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}}|S_{k_{1}k_{3}}^{\rm good}|\lesssim N_{3},\end{split}

(2.55)

where we used (
2.54
) and Lemma
2.7
.

The last estimate of (
2.52
) can be handled similarly as in (
2.55
). Thus, we omit the details.
∎

We also record the following result, which is a consequence of Lemma
2.11
.

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}
be the base tensor defined in (
2.44
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{\Gamma}}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{\min}N_{\med},\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{\Gamma}}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{1}\to kk_{2}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{\med},\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{\Gamma}}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{3}\to kk_{1}k_{2}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{\med},\\
\|\mathbf{1}_{B_{\Gamma}}{\rm T}^{{\rm b},m}_{kk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\|_{k_{2}\to kk_{1}k_{3}}^{2}&\lesssim N_{\med}.\end{split}

In the rest of this subsection,
we recall some bilinear tensor estimates from
[
19
, Proposition 4.11]
, which serve as “nonlinear” estimates for operator norms of tensors.

Proposition 2.17
.

Consider two tensors
h_{k_{A_{1}}}^{(1)}
and

h_{k_{A_{2}}}^{(2)}
, where
A_{1}\cap A_{2}=C
. Let
A_{1}\Delta A_{2}=A
and
define the semi-product

H_{k_{A}}=\sum_{k_{C}}h_{k_{A_{1}}}^{(1)}h_{k_{A_{2}}}^{(2)}.

For any partition
(X,Y)
of
A
, let
X\cap A_{i}=X_{i}
,
Y\cap A_{i}=Y_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Then, we have

\|H\|_{k_{X}\to k_{Y}}\leq\|h^{(1)}\|_{k_{X_{1}\cup C}\to k_{Y_{1}}}\cdot\|h^{(2)}\|_{k_{X_{2}}\to k_{C\cup Y_{2}}}.

The following estimate for special tensor semi-products can be proved using Proposition
2.17
. However, we demonstrate a fundamental proof to showcase the idea of proving Proposition
2.17
.

Lemma 2.18
.

The following holds.

\|h^{(1)}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}h^{(2)}_{k_{2}k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}\to k_{1}k_{2}}\leq\|h^{(1)}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}\to k_{1}}\|h^{(2)}_{k_{2}k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k^{\prime}_{1}\to k_{2}}.

Proof.

By using the definition of the tensor norm (
2.1
), we have

\begin{split}\|h^{(1)}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}h^{(2)}_{k_{2}k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}\to k_{1}k_{2}}&=\sup_{\|a_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}}=1}\bigg\|\sum_{k_{1}^{\prime}}h^{(1)}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}h^{(2)}_{k_{2}k_{1}^{\prime}}a_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\bigg\|_{k_{1}k_{2}}\\
&\leq\|h^{(1)}_{k_{1}k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}\to k_{1}}\sup_{\|a_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k_{1}^{\prime}}=1}\|h^{(2)}_{k_{2}k_{1}^{\prime}}a_{k_{1}^{\prime}}\|_{k^{\prime}_{1}k_{2}}\\
